# A   robust   spline   approach   in   partially   linear   additive   models

Graciela Boente a , ∗ ,   Alejandra   Mercedes Martínez b

- a CONICET and Universidad de Buenos Aires, Argentina
- b CONICET and Universidad Nacional de Luján, Argentina


# a   r   t   i   c   l   e   i   n   f   o

Article   history: Received   7   August   2021 Received   in   revised   form   28   July   2022 Accepted   2   September   2022 Available   online   7   September   2022

2022

Keywords: B -splines Partially   linear   additive   models Robust   estimation

Robust estimation

# a   b   s   t   r   a   c   t

Partially   linear   additive   models   generalize   linear   regression   models   by   assuming   that   the   relationship   between   the   response   and   a   set   of   explanatory   variables   is   linear   on   some   of   the   covariates,   while   the   other   ones   enter   into   the   model   through   unknown   univariate   smooth   functions.   The   harmful   effect   of   outliers   either   in   the   residuals   or   in   the   covariates   involved   in   the   linear   component   has   been   described   in   the   situation   of   partially   linear   models,   that   is,   when   only   one   nonparametric   component   is   involved.   When   dealing   with   additive   components,   the   problem   of   providing   reliable   estimators   when   atypical   data   arise   is   of   practical   importance   motivating   the   need   of   robust   procedures.   Based   on   this   fact,   a   family   of   robust   estimators   for   partially   linear   additive   models   that   combines   B splines   with   robust   linear   MM -regression   estimators   is   proposed.   Under   mild   assumptions,   consistency   results   and   rates   of   convergence   for   the   proposed   estimators   are   derived.   Furthermore,   the   asymptotic   normality   for   the   linear   regression   estimators   is   obtained.   A   Monte   Carlo   study   is   carried   out   to   compare,   under   different   models   and   contamination   schemes,   the   performance   of   the   robust   MM -proposal   based   on   B -splines   with   its   classical   counterpart   and   also   with   a   quantile   approach.   The   obtained   results   show   the   beneﬁts   of   using   the   robust   MM -approach.   The   analysis   of   a   real   data   set   illustrates   the   usefulness   of   the   proposed   method.

# 1.   Introduction

Different   approaches   have   been   considered   in   the   literature   to   deal   with   the   well-known   “curse   of   dimensionality” of   fully   nonparametric   regression   models.   Among   others,   we   can   mention   additive,   single–index,   varying   coeﬃcient   and   partial   linear   models.   Speciﬁcally,   partial   linear   models   allow   the   response   variable   to   depend   linearly   on   some   covariates,   while   the   others   are   modeled in   a   fully   non-parametric   way.   More   precisely,   in   such   models   we   deal   with   observations   ( Y i ,   Z t i ,   X t i ) t independent   and   identically   distributed   (i.i.d.)   with   the   same   distribution   as   ( Y ,   Z t ,   X t ) t where   Y ∈ R ,   Z   ∈ R q and   X   ∈ R p .   The   response   and   covariates   are   related   through

$$
Y = m ( Z , \mathbf X ) + \sigma \, \varepsilon = \beta ^ { \tau } Z + \eta ( \mathbf X ) + \sigma \, \varepsilon \, ,
$$

[Page 2]

where   the   error   ε is   independent   from   ( Z t ,   X t ) t ,   β ∈ R q is   the   regression   parameter   and   the   unknown   multivariate   function   η : R p → R is   assumed   to   be   smooth.   Furthermore,   in   the   classical   setting,   it   is   usually   assumed   that   E ( ε )   = 0 and   Var ( ε )   = 1,   so   σ > 0 stands   for   the   unknown   scale   parameter.

The   particular   situation   where   X ∈ R is   considered   in   Härdle   et   al.   ( 2000 ) and   Härdle   et   al.   ( 2004 ) who   describe   different   procedures   based   on   kernels   or   splines   to   estimate   the   unknown   quantities.   As   in   linear   regression   models,   these   estimators   are   very   sensitive   to   atypical   observations   since   they   are   based   on   the   least   squares   principle.   When   p   = 1 and   to   deal   with   more   reliable   procedures   when   atypical   data   arise,   Bianco   and   Boente   ( 2004 ) considered   robust   estimators   based   on   local   M -estimators,   while   He   and   Shi   ( 1996 ) and   He   et   al.   ( 2002 ) studied   M -estimators   based   on   splines.

It   is   worth   mentioning   that,   when   p   > 1,   model   ( 1 ) requires   multivariate   smoothing,   so   that   the   “curse   of   dimensionality” is   not   overcome   specially   when   p   ≥ 4.   Partially   linear   additive   regression   models   ( plam )   provide   an   attempt   to   solve   this   problem,   since   the   covariates   related   to   the   nonparametric   component   enter   the   model   through   an   additive   structure,   leading   to   the   relationship

$$
Y = \beta ^ { \mathbb { T } } Z + \eta ( X ) + \sigma \, \varepsilon = \mu + \beta ^ { \mathbb { T } } Z + \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { p } \eta _ { j } ( X _ { j } ) + \sigma \, \varepsilon \, , \\ \intertext { w h o r $ \, \sigma \, \text {univariate} $ unknown, }
$$

where   the   univariate   unknown   functions   η j (1   ≤ j   ≤ p ),   the   coeﬃcients   μ   ∈ R and   β ∈ R q and   the   scale   parameter   σ > 0 are   the   quantities   to   estimate.   Usually,   the   functions   η j are   assumed   to   be   continuous   with   support   on   a   compact   interval   I j which   is   also   the   support   of   the   distribution   of   X j .   To   guarantee   identiﬁability,   additional   constraints   on   the   functions   η j are   required.   Some   of   the   most   common   conditions   consist   in   assuming   that     I j η j ( x )   dx   = 0,   for   j   = 1 ,   ...,   p ,   or   E η j ( X j )   = 0,   for   j   = 1 ,   ...,   p . The   partially   linear   additive   regression   model   ( 2 ) includes   the   additive   model   when   the   regression   parameter   equals   zero  

and   the   linear   regression   model   when   η j ≡ 0,   for   all   the   additive   components.   As   mentioned   in   Ma   ( 2012 ),   model   ( 2 ) is   more   parsimonious   and   easier   to   interpret   than   purely   additive   models   and   more   ﬂexible   than   linear   models.   In   this   sense,   the   advantage   over   fully   nonparametric   models,   where   the   covariates   are   usually   assumed   to   have   a   density   with   compact   support,   is   that   through   a   partially   linear   additive   regression   model   the   practitioner   may   include   in   the   linear   regression   component   covariates   which   are   discrete,   dummy   or   unbounded.   Besides   the   situation   where   a   subset   or   all   the   variables   in   Z are   discrete,   partially   linear   additive   regression   models   are   suitable   when   the   practitioner   is   fairly   certain   of   the   linear   relation   existing   between   the   response   and   some   covariates,   but   not   about   the   shape   of   the   relationship   with   the   other   covariates.   We   refer   to   Opsomer   and   Ruppert   ( 1999 ) for   a   discussion   on   this   topic.

Stone   ( 1985 ) discussed   different   issues,   such   as   model   dimensionality   and   ﬂexibility,   for   different   nonparametric   and   semiparametric   models   including   the   partially   linear   additive   regression   model   ( 2 ).   To   estimate   the   components   of   the   model,   Fan   et   al.   ( 1998 ) introduced   two   least   squares   kernel   approaches,   which   have   a   high   computational   cost,   since   they   perform   sequential   estimations   in   the   direction   of   interest,   see   also   Härdle   et   al.   ( 2004 ).   A   related   kernel   based   method   that,   at   the   initial   stage   when   estimating   β ,   makes   use   both   of   the   additive   structure   of   the   nonparametric   component   and   of   the   fact   that   Z enters   in   the   regression   function   linearly,   was   considered   in   Fan   and   Li   ( 2003 ).   A   different   family   of   kernel   based   estimators,   that   reduces   the   computational   requirement,   was   studied   in   Manzan   and   Zerom   ( 2005 ),   while   Liang   et   al.   ( 2008 ) considered   the   situation   where   the   linear   covariate   is   measured   with   error.   Following   a   different   approach,   Opsomer   and   Ruppert   ( 1999 ) combined   local   linear   smoothers   and   backﬁtting   to   provide   a   root − n consistent   estimator   of   β .   In   this   direction,   a   robust   algorithm   based   on   backﬁtting   and   the   generalized   Speckman   estimator   was   considered   in   Pan   ( 2013 ) for   generalized   partial   linear   additive   models.   However,   no   asymptotic   properties   were   derived   for   this   procedure.

The   described   methods   do   not   estimate   the   parameters   and   nonparametric   components   simultaneously.   For   that   reason   and   following   the   spline   approach   given   in   Stone   ( 1985 ) for   additive   models,   Li   ( 2000 ) studied   estimators   based   on   general   series   methods,   with   power   series   and   splines   as   a   particular   case.   To   obtain   estimators   of   the   additive   components   whose   asymptotic   distribution   can   be   derived,   Ma   and   Yang   ( 2011 ) introduced   an   approach   that   combines   spline   estimation   and   kernel   methods.   In   the   context   of   high-dimensional   sparse   models,   Liu   et   al.   ( 2011 ) and   Lian   ( 2012 ) developed   a   variable   selection   procedure   based   on   least   squares   regression   and   spline   approximations,   using   SCAD   and   adaptive   LASSO   penalizations   on   the   regression   parameter   β ,   respectively.

All   these   estimators   are   based   on   a   least   squares   approach   and   assume   that   the   error   has   ﬁnite   variance,   so,   as   in   partial   linear   models,   a   small   proportion   of   atypical   data   may   seriously   affect   the   estimations.   A   more   resistant   approach   based   on   quantile   regression   and   spline   approximation   was   suggested   in   Guo   et   al.   ( 2013 ) and   Sherwood   and   Wang   ( 2016 ) who   also   considered   variable   selection   in   sparse   models.   An   extension   to   censored   partially   linear   additive   models   was   studied   in   Liu   et   al.   ( 2017 ),   while   an   approach   based   on   penalized   splines   was   discussed   in   Koenker   ( 2011 ).   Note   that   quantile   estimators   are   related   to   an   unbounded   loss   function   and   for   that   reason,   as   in   linear   regression   models,   they   may   be   affected   by   high–leverage   outliers.   In   particular,   when   considering   a   0.5   quantile,   quantile   regression   estimators   are   related   to   the   well   known   least   absolute   deviations   estimators.

To overcome the sensitivity of quantile estimators to high-leverage outliers, we adapt best practices for robust multiple linear regression estimators to partial linear additive models. More precisely, we propose estimators that combine B -splines to approximate the additive components with MM -regression estimators (Yohai, 1987). As it is well known, MM -regression estimators are a family of scale equivariant estimators, robust against high-leverage outliers since they are obtained using a bounded loss function in the minimization problem and a preliminary robust high breakdown point scale estimator. This will allow that, in our approach, the error's distribution may have heavy tails since, instead of requiring E (ε) = 0 and Var (ε) = 1 as in the classical setting, we only require that the error ε has a symmetric distribution F ( · ) . The scale parameter of F ( · ) is assumed to be 1 to identify σ . In this way, the resulting estimators are strongly consistent under standard regularity conditions and can be calibrated so that the regression estimators achieve high efficiency for normal errors. Moreover, as the numerical results reported in Section 5 show, our proposal leads to estimators resistant to vertical outliers and to atypical data with high-leverage in the covariates corresponding to the linear component.

[Page 3]

In   this   context,   it   should   be   mentioned   that   in   many   applications   categorical   variables   may   arise.   The   problem   on   how   to   deal   with   discrete   variables   in   fully   nonparametric   regression   problems   has   been   considered   in   the   statistical   literature,   by   several   authors.   Among   others,   we   can   mention   the   proposals   based   on   splines   for   the   continuous   covariates   and   on   discrete-support   kernel   functions   for   the   categorical   ones,   considered   in   Racine   and   Li   ( 2004 ) and   Ma   et   al.   ( 2015 ) for   nonparametric   regression   models   and   by   Ma   and   Racine   ( 2013 ) for   additive   models.   As   mentioned   in   Camlong-Viot   et   al.   ( 2006 ),   who   considered   a   kernel   approach   combined   with   marginal   integration,   in   the   econometric   literature   categorical   covariates   are   usually   included   in   the   form   of   linear   parametric   functions,   see   also   Ma   and   Yang   ( 2011 ).   Throughout   this   paper,   as   in   Ma   and   Yang   ( 2011 ) who   considered   a   least   squares   approach   or   Guo   et   al.   ( 2013 ) and   Sherwood   and   Wang   ( 2016 ) whose   proposal   is   based   on   quantile   regression,   we   will   assume   that   discrete   variables   if   present   are   included   in   the   model   as   some   of   the   components   of   the   vector   Z whose   relation   with   the   responses   is   modeled parametrically.

The   rest   of   the   paper   is   organized   as   follows.   In   Section 2 ,   we   describe   our   robust   proposal,   while   theoretical   results   regarding   consistency   and   rates   of   convergence   are   presented   in   Section 3 .   Furthermore,   asymptotic   normality   is   established   for   the   estimators   of   β in   Section 4 .   The   results   of   a   numerical   study   conducted   to   compare   the   ﬁnite-sample   properties   of   the   classical   least   squares   and   the   robust   MM -estimator   are   summarized   in   Section 5 ,   while   Section 6 contains   the   analysis   of   the   Boston   housing   data   set.   Some   ﬁnal   comments   are   presented   in   Section 7 .   All   proofs   are   relegated   to   the   Appendix   or   to   the   supplementary   ﬁle   available   on-line,   where   some   additional   numerical   results   are   also   included.

# 2.   The   robust   estimators

As   mentioned   in   the   introduction,   we   will   consider   i.i.d.   observations   ( Y i ,   Z t i ,   X t i ) t satisfying   the   partially   linear   additive   model   ( 2 ) where   X i has   support   on     p j = 1 I j .   From   now   on,   given   two   random   vectors   W   ∈ R s and   V   ∈ R s ,   we   denote   W   ∼ V whenever   W and   V have   the   same   distribution.   In   the   sequel,   ( Y ,   Z t ,   X t ) t stands   for   a   random   vector   with   the   same   distribution   as   ( Y 1 ,   Z t 1 ,   X t 1 ) t ,   that   is,   ( Y ,   Z t ,   X t ) t ∼ ( Y 1 ,   Z t 1 ,   X t 1 ) t .   To   ensure   identiﬁability   of   the   additive   components   η j : I j → R ,   we   will   impose   the   constraint     I j η j ( x )   dx   = 0,   for   j   = 1 ,   ...,   p .   The   errors   ε are   assumed   to   be   independent   of   the   explanatory   variables   ( Z t ,   X t ) t .   Taking   into   account   that   we   intend   to   deﬁne   robust   estimators,   we   avoid   moment   conditions   for   the   error’s   distribution   by   only   assuming   that   their   distribution   F ( · ) is   symmetric   with   scale   parameter   1. Our   robust   proposal   combines   B -splines   with   regression   MM -estimators,   that   is,   the   estimators   to   be   deﬁned   in   Sec-

tion 2.2 are   based   on   MM -regression   estimators   after   approximating   each   component   η j by   a   spline   as   described   in   Section 2.1 .   Under   a   linear   regression   model,   MM -estimators   were   introduced   in   Yohai   ( 1987 ).   A   detailed   description,   as   well   as   an   algorithm   allowing   to   compute   them,   can   be   seen   in   Maronna   et   al.   ( 2019 ).   As   it   is   well   known,   to   attain   robustness   and   eﬃciency,   MM -estimators   are   deﬁned   through   a   two–step   procedure.   In   the   ﬁrst   step,   a   preliminary   robust   regression   estimator   is   used   to   estimate   the   scale   parameter.   This   initial   estimator   may   be   ineﬃcient   but   should   have   a   high   breakdown   point.   In   the   second   step,   an   M -estimator   is   computed   using   a   bounded   loss   function   applied   to   the   standardized   residuals.   In   this   way,   the   ﬁnal   estimator   will   retain   the   high   breakdown   point   of   the   initial   one   but   its   eﬃciency   will   be   improved   by   the   use   of   an   appropriate   loss   function   in   the   second   step.   The   next   two   sections   contain   the   details   of   our   proposal.

# 2.1.   B-spline   approximation   of   the   additive   components

To   approximate   each   additive   function   η j in   ( 2 ) by   a   spline,   ﬁx   the   desired   spline   order     j ,   the   number   of   interior   knots   N n , j and   their   location   T j .   As   it   is   well   known,   the   class   of   splines   of   order     j > 1 with   knots   T j is   spanned   by   a   B -spline   basis   functions   with   dimension   k j = k n , j = N n , j +   j ,   see   Corollary   4.10   of   Schumaker   ( 1981 ).   We   will   denote   this   basis   {   B ( j ) s : 1   ≤ s   ≤ k n , j } .   It   is   worth   mentioning   that   a   spline   of   order     is   a   polynomial   of   degree       − 1 within   each   subinterval.   It   should   be   noted   that   when   using   cubic   splines,   the   consistency   results   derived   in   Section 3 hold   when   the   additive   components   are   twice   continuously   differentiable. ( j ) ( j ) ( j ) t k k j ( j ) ( j )

To   deﬁne   the   estimators,   for   any   vector   λ = (λ 1 ,   ...,   λ k j ) ∈ R j ,   we   consider   a   spline   g j ( x )   =   s = 1 λ s   B s ( x ) to   approximate   η j ,   for   1   ≤ j   ≤ p .   It   is   worth   noticing   that   even   when   considering   the   classical   least   squares   estimators,   the   minimization   should   be   carried   out   taking   into   account   the   constraints     I j g j ( x )   dx   =   k j s = 1 λ ( j ) s   I j   B ( j ) s ( x )   dx   = 0,   for   j   = 1 ,   ...,   p .   As   in   Guo   et   al.   ( 2013 ),   to   deal   with   an   unconstrained   optimization   problem,   we   center   the   basis   functions,   that   is,   we   deﬁne   B ( j ) s ( x )   =   B ( j ) s ( x )   −   I j   B ( j ) s ( x ) dx and   the   centered   approximation   candidates   for   η j as     k j s = 1 λ ( j ) s B ( j ) s ( x ) . 3

# 2.2.   The   robust   MM-estimators

In   what   follows   the   loss   functions   ρ 0 and   ρ 1 to   be   considered   below   will   be   bounded   ρ -functions   as   deﬁned   in   Maronna   et   al.   ( 2019 ),   so     ρ 0   ∞ =   ρ 1   ∞ = 1 (see   assumption C1(a) ).   A   widely   used   family   of   bounded   ρ -functions   is   the   Tukey’s   bisquare   function   which   is   of   the   form   ρ t , c ( t )   = ρ t ( t / c ) where   ρ t ( t )   = min   1 − ( 1 − t 2 ) 3 , 1   ,   that   is,   ρ t , c ( t )   = min   1 − ( 1 − ( t / c ) 2 ) 3 , 1   .   The   tuning   constant   c > 0 balances   the   robustness   and   eﬃciency   properties   of   the   associated   estimators. To   deﬁne   the   robust   estimators,   as   in   linear   regression,   we   ﬁrst   compute   an   S -estimator   using   ρ 0 .   This   preliminary

For t h e a r t h a r e o n , b = 1 / 2 i s h o s e n o t b a t i n }

Since     ρ 0   ∞ = 1,   the   breakdown   point   of   the   M -scale   estimator   is   min ( b ,   1   − b ) .   For   that   reason,   b   = 1 / 2 is   chosen   to   obtain   a   50%   breakdown   point   for   the   scale   estimator.   Furthermore,   to   guarantee   Fisher–consistency,   we   need   that   b   = E ( ρ 0 ( ε )) .   As   mentioned   in   Maronna   et   al.   ( 2019 ),   typically   the   function   ρ 0 depends   on   a   tuning   constant   c 0 ,   that   is,   ρ 0 ( t )   = ρ ( t / c 0 ) with   ρ a   ρ -function   and   c 0 > 0.   In   linear   regression   models,   the   constant   c 0 is   numerically   selected   by   the   user   to   guarantee   Fisher–consistency   at   a   given   distribution   for   a   chosen   breakdown   point.   For   instance,   when   ρ 0 is   the   bisquare   function,   that   is,   when   ρ 0 ( t )   = ρ t ( t / c 0 ) ,   we   may   choose   the   tuning   constant   c 0 as   c 0 = 1 . 54764 to   ensure   Fisher–consistency   when   ε ∼ N ( 0 ,   1 ) and   breakdown   point   b   = 1 / 2.   As   described   in   Maronna   et   al. Step 3 Choose a bounded ρ -function ρ 0.

(a) Compute the M -scale of the residuals r i ( a , b , c ) , 1 ≤ i ≤ n , related to ρ 0, as defined in (4) and denote it sn ( a , b , c ) .

(b) Minimize the scale sn ( a , b , c ) , to obtain the scale estimator ̂ σ as its minimum value. Step 4 Choose ρ 1 such that ρ 1 ≤ ρ 0 and sup t ρ 1 ( t ) = sup t ρ 0 ( t ) . Using ρ 1, compute an M -estimator ( ̂ µ, ̂ β , ̂ c ) with preliminary scale estimator ̂ σ , as defined in (6). The estimators of the additive components are then obtained using the coefficients c through (7).

̂ With respect to the numerical implementation of our proposal, once the centered B -spline { B ( j ) s ( x ) } k j -1 s = 1 are obtained in Step 1 , for j = 1 , . . . , p and the pseudo-covariates V i are computed as described in Step 2 , the estimators in Step 4 may be easily obtained using the function lmrob of the library robustbase in R which returns the estimated coefficients ̂ µ , ̂ β and c as well as the scale estimator σ from Step 3 .

̂ ̂ As mentioned above, once the centered B -spline bases are constructed, our proposal is obtained using MM -regression estimators. For that reason, if the basis dimensions, k j , 1 ≤ j ≤ p , are known and fi xed, our proposal leads to estimators of β resistant to high-leverage outliers and with high breakdown point, see Yohai (1987) and Maronna et al. (2019). However, in practice, the number of elements of the basis k j is chosen from the data, for instance, using a robust BIC criterion as the one described in Section 2.3. The breakdown point of the proposed B -spline MM -estimator when the dimension is data-driven is beyond the scope of the paper and this interesting topic may be object of future work.

# 2.3.   Selection   of   k j

To   implement   the   proposed   B -spline   MM -estimators,   the   selection   of   the   knot   sequence   to   be   used   when   estimating   the   j -th   additive   component   is   an   important   topic.   Clearly,   knot   selection   is   more   relevant   when   estimating   η j than   for   the   estimators   of   β .   As   mentioned   by   Stone   ( 1985 ),   the   number   of   knots   is   more   crucial   than   their   location   and   for   that   reason   we   discuss   below   an   approach   to   select   the   number   of   interior   knots   N n , j or,   equivalently,   the   basis   dimension   k j ,   using   a   robust   BIC   criterion.   Regarding   the   knots   location,   equally   spaced   knots   or   quantile   knots   are   two   possible   choices.   Uniform   knots   are   usually   used   when   the   function   η j does   not   exhibit   dramatic   changes   in   its   derivatives.   In   contrast,   non–uniform   knots   are   desirable   when   the   function   has   very   different   local   behaviors in   different   regions.   A   commonly   used   approach   in   this   last   situation   is   to   consider   quantile   knots,   that   is,   the   quantiles   of   the   observed   explanatory   variables   X ij ,   1   ≤ i   ≤ n ,   with   uniform   percentile   ranks.

The   number   of   elements   of   the   basis   k j which   approximates   each   additive   function   may   be   determined   by   a   model   selection   criterion.   However,   it   is   well   known   that,   to   ensure   robustness   properties   of   the   ﬁnal   estimator,   a   robust   criterion   is   needed.   As   in   He   et   al.   ( 2002 ),   a   robust   BIC criterion   may   be   deﬁned   as   follows

$$
i \text { needed. As in He et al. When   all   the   additive   components   η j are   twice   continuously   differentiable   with   second   derivative   Lipschitz   of   order   1   and   cubic   splines   are   considered   for   each   component,   that   is,     j = 4 for   j   = 1 ,   ...,   p ,   Theorem 3.3 and   assumption C4 below   show   that   the   rate   for   the   bases   dimension   is   almost   n 1 / 5 (see   also   Remark 3.2 ). The   numerical   complexity   is   reduced   if,   as   in   our   simulation   study,   the   size   of   the   basis   is   the   same   for   all   j ,   in   which   case,   one   looks   for   the   ﬁrst   local   minimum   of   RBIC ( k ) deﬁned   through   ( 9 ),   when   k   ∈ N varies   in   [ max ( n 1 / 5 / 2 ,   4 ),   8   + 2   n 1 / 5 ] .

# 2.4.   Regarding   some   other   basis   choices

The   MM -estimators   deﬁned   above   are   based   on   B -splines   which   provides   a   ﬂexible   basis,   often   accurate   and   computational   convenient.   Another   advantage   of   B -splines   basis   is   that   they   allow   for   uniform   approximations   of   the   additive   components.   Indeed,   as   mentioned   in   Stone   ( 1985 ),   B -splines   basis   functions   can   be   used   to   effectively   approximate   sufﬁciently   smooth   functions.   To   be   more   precise,   if   the   function   η j is   continuously   differentiable   of   order   r j with   Lipschitz   r j -derivative   (see   assumption C3 below),   according   to   Corollary   6.21   in   Schumaker   ( 1981 ) there   exists   a   spline   of   order     j ,     η j ( x )   =   k j j = 1 λ ( j ) s   B ( j ) s ( x ) such   that   η j − η j   ∞ = O   k − r j j   . (10)

  Approximation   ( 10 ) is   a   key   point   when   deriving   uniform   consistency   results   for   the   MM -estimators   of   η j in   Theorem 3.2 . Clearly,   other   basis   may   be   used   in Step   1 instead   of   B -splines   to   generate   k j -ﬁnite   dimensional   candidates.   However   to   obtain   analogous   results   to   those   provided   in   Theorem 3.2 ,   uniform   approximations   are   needed.   In   this   direction,   the   papers   by   Newey   ( 1997 ) and   Li   ( 2000 ) give   general   results   for   series   function   estimators   by   requiring   that   the   nonparametric   function   may   be   approximated   at   a   given   rate.

Among   others,   Bernstein   polynomials   are   a   possible   alternative   to   B -splines.   Recall   that   of   Bernstein   polynomials   of   order   k j are   deﬁned   taking   in Step   1

In   particular,   Theorem   3.2   in   Powell   ( 1981 ) ensures   that   Bernstein   polynomials   of   order   k j provide   uniform   approximations   as   those   given   in   ( 10 ).   It   is   worth   mentioning   that,   as   B -splines,   Bernstein   polynomials   are   such   that     k j s = 0   B ( j ) s ( x )   = 1,   for   all   x   ∈ I j .   Hence,   after   the   centered   basis   B ( j ) s ( x )   =   B ( j ) s ( x )   −   I j   B ( j ) s ( x ) dx ,   s   = 0 ,   ...,   k j ,   is   obtained   in Step   1 ,   the   elements   of   the   basis   are   still   linearly   dependent.   For   that   reason,   in Step   1 (d),   V ( j ) ( t ) should   be   deﬁned   omitting   the   term   corresponding   to   s   = 0,   that   is,   V ( j ) ( t )   = ( B ( j ) 1 ( t ),   ...,   B ( j ) k j ( t )) t .   Therefore,   the   effective   dimension   of   the   linear   space   used   to   approximate   the   nonparametric   additive   components   equals   K =   p j = 1 k j . Legendre   polynomials   are   a   well   known   orthogonal   basis,   extensively   employed   in   approximation   theory.   The   convergence   rates   obtained   in   Wang   and   Xiang   ( 2012 ) may   be   used   to   derive   uniform   consistency   results   of   the   considered  

Legendre polynomials are a well known orthogonal basis, extensively employed in approximation theory. The convergence rates obtained in Wang and Xiang (2012) may be used to derive uniform consistency results of the considered MM -estimators. However, it should be taken into account that in this case, the approximation rate in (10) is O ( k -r j + 1 / 2 j )

instead of O ( k -r j j ) , modifying the dimension basis requirements stated in Section 3 as well as the convergence rates assumed in assumption N2 to derive asymptotic normality results. Legendre polynomials and the Fourier basis to be discussed below, automatically fulfill the condition ∫ I j ˜ B ( j ) s ( x ) dx = 0, for s ≥ 2, since they are orthogonal bases with fi rst element ˜ B ( j ) 1 ≡ 1. Thus, we can take B ( j ) s = ˜ B ( j ) s + 1 , for s = 1 , . . . , k j -1, in Step 1 (c) and (d). In this situation the effective dimension is the same as for B -splines.

periodic functions. As mentioned in Newey ( 1997 ), this basis is usually not considered in most econometric applications. One   drawback   of   Fourier   basis   is   that,   even   to   obtain   pointwise   approximations,   additional   assumptions   such   as   the   Dini

# 3.   Consistency   results

This   section   aims   to   obtain   results   concerning   the   consistency   and   the   convergence   rates   of   the   estimators   deﬁned   in   Section 2 under   assumptions C1 to C7 below.   As   in   Guo   et   al.   ( 2013 ),   without   loss   of   generality,   we   will   assume   I j = [ 0 ,   1 ] for   j   = 1 ,   ...,   p .   In   assumption C1 below   the   function   ρ will   correspond   to   either   ρ 0 or   ρ 1 according   to   the   result   to   be   derived.   From   now   on   C r ( 0 ,   1 ) will   stand   for   the   space   of   functions   continuously   differentiable   up   to   order   r ,       ·   refers   to   the   Euclidean   norm   in   R q and   for   any   continuous   function   v   : R   → R ,     v   ∞ = sup t | v ( t ) | .   We   will   denote   as   G the   class   of   functions   G = { g : [ 0 ,   1 ]   → R such that   1 0 g ( x ) dx   = 0 } and,   for   any   r ≥ 1,   we   deﬁne η r 0 , 1 η ( ) < , 0   r and sup | η ( r ) ( z 1 ) − η ( r ) ( z 2 ) | < .

$$
\mathcal { H } _ { r } = \{ \eta \in \mathcal { C } ^ { r } [ 0 , 1 ] \, \colon \, \| \eta ^ { ( \ell ) } \| _ { \infty } < \infty , \, 0 \leq \ell \leq r \text { and } \sup _ { z _ { 1 } \neq z _ { 2 } } \frac { | \eta ^ { ( r ) } ( z _ { 1 } ) - \eta ^ { ( r ) } ( z _ { 2 } ) | } { | z _ { 1 } - z _ { 2 } | } < \infty \} \, .
$$

C1 (a) The   function   ρ : R   → [ 0 ;   +∞ ) is   bounded,   continuous,   even,   non–decreasing   in   [ 0 ,   +∞ ) and   such   that   ρ ( 0 )   = 0.   Furthermore,   lim t →+∞ ρ ( t )    = 0 and   if   0   ≤ u   < v with   ρ ( v )   < sup t ρ ( t ) then   ρ ( u )   < ρ ( v ) . (b) ρ is continuously differentiable with bounded derivative ψ . Moreover, the function ζ : R → R defined as ζ( t ) = t ψ( t ) , t ∈ R , is bounded.

- C2 The random variable ε has a density function f 0 ( t ) that is even, monotone non-decreasing in | t | , and strictly decreasing for | t | in a neighborhood of 0.
- C3 For 1 ≤ j ≤ p , the true function η j ∈ H r j where r j ≥ 1. Furthermore, the splines order used to estimate η j satisfy /lscript j ≥ r j + 2.

Conditions C1 and C2 are   standard   conditions   for   the   errors   and   for   the   loss   function,   respectively.   The   ﬁrst  


Remark 3.1. Conditions C1 and C2 are standard conditions for the errors and for the loss function, respectively. The fi rst one is a condition assumed in the context of robustness to ensure Fisher-consistency. In this sense, C6 is also a requirement for Fisher-consistency and it is the conditional counterpart of the usual assumption in linear regression models to guarantee Fisher-consistency. Under the partially linear additive model we are considering, Fisher-consistency will be derived in Lemma A.1 in the Appendix. Note that if, for almost any x 0 ∈ R p , the distribution of Z given X = x 0 has a density, then P ( b t Z = a | X = x 0 ) = 0, for any a ∈ R , b ∈ R q , ( b , a ) /negationslash= 0, implying that C6 and C7 hold. Furthermore, it is worth mentioning that C6 holds whenever P ( b t Z + ∑ p j = 1 g j ( X j ) = a ) = 0 in C7 .

Condition C3 regards the smoothness of the additive nonparametric components and r j corresponds to the smoothness degree of the j -additive true functions η j . The regularity of the additive components stated in C3 is related to the order of the B -splines used to approximate them, meaning that if for instance cubic splines are used, our results will be valid for twice continuously differentiable functions. As mentioned in He et al. (2002), if we think that η j is less smooth, quadratic splines can be considered.

The   condition   about   the   knots   spacing   given   in C4 is   a   standard   one   when   using   B -spline   approximations.

Strong   consistency   of   the   preliminary   scale   estimator   is   required   in C5 to   allow   for   other   choices   of   the   robust   scale   estimators,   besides   the   one   introduced   in   Section 2.2 .   Proposition 3.1 below   states   that   the   S -scale   deﬁned   through   ( 5 ) is   indeed   strongly   consistent   as   required   in C5 .

Proposition 3.1 derives   strong   consistency   results   for   the   residual   scale   estimator     σ deﬁned   through   ( 5 ).   To   derive   this   result   we   deﬁne   the   population   counterpart   of     σ .   More   precisely,   given   a   ∈ R ,   b   ∈ R q and   g j ∈ G ,   1   ≤ j   ≤ p ,   S ( a ,   b ,   g 1 ,   ...,   g p ) stands   for   the   M -scale   functional   corresponding   to   the   residuals   r ( a ,   b ,   g 1 ,   ...,   g p )   = Y − a   − b t Z   −   p j = 1 g j ( X j ) ,   that   is,   S ( a ,   b ,   g 1 ,   ...,   g p ) satisﬁes

$$
\mathbb { E } \rho _ { 0 } \left ( \frac { r ( a , b , g _ { 1 } , \dots , g _ { p } ) } { S ( a , b , g _ { 1 } , \dots , g _ { p } ) } \right ) = b \, . \\ \text {Henceforth} \, \text { the scale estimators are calibrated}
$$

Henceforth,   the   scale   estimators   are   calibrated   so   that

Theorem 3.3 provides convergence rates with respect to the distance π P . Its proof is relegated to the supplementary fi le. From now on, we denote as λ = min1 ≤ j ≤ p ( r j ν j ) and ν = max1 ≤ j ≤ p ν j , where ν j and r j are given in assumptions C3 and C4 .

Theorem 3.3. Assume that ρ 1 satisfies C1 , ψ 1 is continuously differentiable with bounded derivative and that C2 to C6 and C8 hold. Furthermore, assume that E ‖ Z ‖ 2 < ∞ and that C7 holds with c < 1 -b ρ 1 , where b ρ 1 = L ( θ , σ) < 1 . Let ̂ θ = ( ̂ µ, ̂ β t , ̂ η 1 , . . . , ̂ η p ) t be the estimators defined through (6) and (7) .

Then, for any sequence of positive numbers { γ n } n ∈ N such that γ n = O ( n λ ) and γ n √ log (γ n ) = O ( n ( 1 -ν)/ 2 ) , we have that γ n π P ( ̂ θ , θ ) = O P ( 1 ) . Hence, when ν j = 1 /( 1 + 2 r j ) in C4 , the estimators converge at rate γ n = n ( 1 -ν)/ 2 -ω for ω > 0 arbitrarily small. In particular, when the same smoothness degree r is assumed for all additive components, i.e., r j = r, for all 1 ≤ j ≤ p and ν j = 1 /( 1 + 2 r ) , a convergence rate n r /( 1 + 2 r ) -ω is obtained, leading to a rate arbitrarily close to the optimal one, in terms of the prediction distance.

Under additional assumptions, Corollary 3.4 below provides rates of consistency for the estimators of the regression parameter and the parameter µ , as well as convergence rates for the additive components estimators with respect to the L 2 ( P ) norm, when the estimators and regression functions are centered with respect to their expected values. Its proof is given in the supplementary fi le.

To obtain the asymptotic distribution of ̂ β , we will need the following additional assumptions. N1 The matrix A is non-singular.

N2 For 1 ≤ j ≤ p , ν j = 1 /( 2 r j + 1 ) with r j ≥ 1. Let ̂ θ = ( ̂ µ, ̂ β t , ̂ η 1 , . . . , ̂ η p ) t be the estimators defined through (6) and (7) and γ n = n ( 1 -ν)/ 2 -ω where ν = max1 ≤ j ≤ p ν j and 0 ≤ ω<( 1 -ν)/ 8. One of the following conditions holds

N3 For each 1 ≤ m ≤ q , the function h ∗ m ( x ) is an additive function in x , that is, it can be written as

Remark   4.1.   Condition N1 prevents   any   element   of   Z from   being   a.s.   perfectly   predictable   by   X since,   in   this   case,   the   model   would   be   fully   nonparametric.   Moreover,   it   is   a   standard   requirement   in   robust   regression   to   obtain   rootn estimators   of   the   linear   components.   Assumption N1 together   with N3 entail   that   Z should   not   be   perfectly   predictable   by   a   linear   combination   of   the   components   of   X .   Note   that   the   additive   structure   required   in   assumption N3 is   satisﬁed   if,   for   instance,   Z and   X are   independent   in   which   case   h ∗ mj ≡ 0 or   if   each   covariate   Z m of   Z depends   only   on   one   covariate   X j of   X .   The   smoothness   requirement   in   assumption N3 was   also   a   condition   in   assumption (A8) in   Ma   and   Yang   ( 2011 ).   Finally,   it   should   be   noticed   that   the   rates   of   convergence   required   in N2 may   be   obtained   from   Theorem 3.3 .

From now on, without loss of generality by eventually modifying µ , we will assume that the parameter φ m in N3 equals 0, so we have that h ∗ m ( x ) = ∑ p j = 1 h ∗ mj ( x j ) .

Note   that     A is   an   average   of   the   covariate   residuals   r i , Z = Z i −   h ∗ ( X i ) ,   so   that   large   values   of   them   may   distort   its   value.   A   similar   behavior arises   in   linear   regression   models   and   has   been   discussed   in   Section   5.6   in   Maronna   et   al.   ( 2019 ).   In   our   setting,   the   problem   is   increased   since   high– leverage   observations   may   also   affect   the   estimators     h ∗ of   h ∗ if   not   chosen   appropriately   (see   the   discussion   below),   in   which   case,   all   values   of   r i , Z will   be   distorted.   In   particular,   the   covariate   residuals   related   to   the   outliers   will   be   smaller   than   expected   producing   larger   estimated   asymptotic   variances   for   each   component   of     β .                                           h ∗  

In order to control this effect one may combine the ideas in Yohai et al. ( 1991 ) with a more stable estimator of . To   be   more   precise,   let   w ( t )   = ψ( t )/ t if   t  = 0 and   w ( 0 )   = ψ   ( 0 ) the   weight   function   related   to   the   score   function   ψ and   denote,   for   brevity,   w i = w (     i ) ,   where       i are   deﬁned   in   ( 14 ). The   independence   between   the   covariates   and   the   errors   ensures   that,   under   appropriate   convergence   conditions   for     h ∗ ,     A p −→ A .   Besides,   if     h ∗ is   a   resistant   estimator,   an   observation   with   high–leverage   will   still   have   a   large   residual   Z i −   h ∗ ( X i ) .   The   effect   of   a   bad   leverage   point   will   be   downweighted   by   the   weights   w i which   may   be   0   for   large   values   of   the   residuals,   if   for   instance   the   bisquare   loss   function   is   chosen,   controlling   in   this   way   the   damaging   effect   on   the   estimated   asymptotic   variances.   In   contrast,   if   the   i -th   observation   is   such   that   Z i is   a   good   leverage   point,   that   is,   one   with   a   small   residual       i ,   the   enlargement   effect   of   Z i −   h ∗ ( X i ) will   be   beneﬁcial   on     A reducing   the   asymptotic   variances. An   estimator     h ∗ ( x )   = (   h ∗ 1 ( x ),   ... ,     h ∗ q ( x )) t of   h ∗ ( x ) can   be   deﬁned   as     h ∗ ( x )   = h ∗   φ ,   ξ ( x ) ,   i.e.,     h ∗ m ( x )   = h ∗   φ m ,   ξ m ( x )   =   φ m +   p j = 1   k j − 1 s = 1   ξ ( m , j ) s B ( j ) s ( x j ) where   the   vectors     φ and     ξ minimize   ϒ ( a ,   ξ ) over   a   = ( a 1 ,   ...,   a q ) t and   ξ = ( ξ t 1 ,   ...,   ξ t q ) t .   However,   even   when   this   estimator   is   appropriate   when   no   outliers   arise   in   the   covariates   related   to   the   linear   component   of   the   model,   it   will   not   be   resistant   when   high–leverage   points   are   present.   A   possible   solution   to   solve   this   problem   is   discussed   below   and   uses   also   an   MM -approach.

A   ﬁrst   attempt   to   solve   the   lack   of   robustness   of   the   estimator   that   minimizes   ϒ ( a ,   ξ ) is   to   mimic   the   arguments   considered   in   the   construction   of     A and   to   deﬁne     h ∗ minimizing   a   weighted   version   of   ϒ ( a ,   ξ ) with   weights   { w i } n i = 1 .   However,   even   though   this   proposal   will   control   the   effect   of   bad   leverage   points   providing   consistent   estimators   of   h ∗ ,   good   leverage   points   will   still   inﬂuence   the   estimation   producing   small   values   of   Z i −   h ∗ ( X i ) for   these   observations   in   detriment   to   the   other   observations   that   will   see   their   covariate   residual   r i , Z increased.   For   that   reason,   in   order   to   provide   a   proper   estimator   of   h ∗ ,   we   will   further   assume   that   a   model   relates   Z m with   the   covariates   X 1 ,   ...,   X p ,   see   He   et   al.   ( 2002 ) for   a   related   model.   From   now   on,   we   assume   that   Z im = φ m +   p j = 1 h ∗ mj ( X ij )   + σ m u im ,   where   u im ∼ F m ( · ) are   independent   from   X i and   independent   from   each   other,   for   1   ≤ i   ≤ n ,   σ m > 0 is   the   scale   parameter   and   F m is   symmetric   around   0 with   scale   1.   A   procedure   similar   to   the   one   described   in   Section 2 can   be   implemented   as   follows   leading   to   uniform   consistent   estimators. 1 = =   Note   that,   for   each   m ,   (   φ m ,     ξ m ) can   be   obtained   minimizing   the   quantity     n i = 1 ρ 1   r i , m ( a m , ξ m )/   σ m   . 5.   Monte   Carlo   study

This   section   contains   the   results   of   a   simulation   study   conducted   to   compare,   under   different   models   and   contamination   schemes,   the   performance   of   the   robust   MM -estimators   deﬁned   in   Section 2 with   two   competitors.   All   computations   were   carried   out   in R .   The   code   used   is   available   at   https://github   .com   /alemermartinez   /rplam .   The   classical   counterpart   of   the   MM -estimator   corresponds   to   a   linear   regression   least   squares   estimator   after   the   B -splines   approximation   was   performed   for   each   additive   component.   For   the   robust   MM -estimator,   we   considered   as   loss   functions   ρ 0 and   ρ 1 the   Tukey’s   bisquare   function   with   tuning   constants   c 0 and   c 1 ,   respectively.   More   precisely,   for   the   initial   S -estimators,   we   choose   c 0 = 1 . 54764

[Page 12]

and   b   = 1 / 2,   while   for   the   M -step   the   tuning   constant   equals   c 1 = 4 . 685.   As   mentioned   above,   the   value   c 0 = 1 . 54764 ensures   Fisher–consistency   of   the   scale   estimator   when   the   errors   have   a   normal   distribution.   Besides,   c 1 = 4 . 685 corresponds   to   the   tuning   constant   that   guarantees,   for   normal   errors,   a   95%   eﬃciency   for   the   robust   estimators   of   β (see   Theorem 4.1 and   Maronna   et   al.,   2019 ).   We   have   also   evaluated   the   quantile   estimator   deﬁned   in   Guo   et   al.   ( 2013 ) taking   a   0.5   quantile,   that   is,   we   chose   τ = 1 / 2 in   the   loss   function   ρ τ ( u )   = u ( τ − I ( u < 0 ) ) deﬁning   the   estimators.   This   choice   ensures   that,   for   clean   samples,   the   three   procedures   estimate   the   same   quantities,   otherwise   the   estimator   of   μ will   be   biased.   Note   that,   when   τ = 0 . 5,   the   quantile   estimators   minimize   the   absolute   value   of   the   residuals,   corresponding   to   the   so–called   L 1 or   least   absolute   deviation   estimators.   From   now   on,   we   will   denote   them as l 1 in   all   tables   and   ﬁgures.   Besides,   the   classical   least   squares   estimator   will   be   labeled as ls in   all   tables   and   ﬁgures   and   the   robust   MM -procedure   proposed   in   this   paper   as mm .

In   all   scenarios,   we   performed   N = 500 replications,   the   sample   size   was   n   = 100 and   we   used   cubic   splines   with   equally   spaced   knots   for   all   nonparametric   components.   For   numerical   simplicity,   we   also   chose   the   same   number   of   terms   in   the   spline   approximation   for   each   additive   component,   that   is,   k j = k for   all   j .   As   in   He   et   al.   ( 2002 ),   the   common   number   of   elements   in   the   basis   k varies   between   max { n 1 / 5 / 2 ;   4 } and   8   + 2 n 1 / 5 ,   which   for   the   sample   size   considered   leads   to   4   ≤ k   ≤ 13.   To   select   the   basis   dimension,   we   minimized   the   RBIC criteria   deﬁned   in   ( 8 ) over   the   set   { 4 ,   ...,   13 } with   the   same   ρ -function   considered   in   the   estimation   step.   Hence,   for   the   robust   estimator,   the   ρ -function   in   ( 8 ) is   the   Tukey’s   bisquare   loss   function   with   tuning   constant   c 1 = 4 . 685,   while   for   the   classical   and   L 1 estimators,   ρ ( t )   = t 2 and   ρ ( t )   = | t | ,   respectively.   Note   that   for   the   classical   and   L 1 procedures   no   scale   estimator   is   needed. The   samples   Y Z t X t t n are   generated   with   the   same   distribution   as   Y   Z t   X t t ,   X   X   X t 2 ,   Z

In   all   cases,   the   response   and   the   covariates   are   related   through   the   partially   linear   additive   model   Y = μ   + β t Z   + η 1 ( X 1 )   + η 2 ( X 2 )   + U ,   where   U = σ ε ,   σ = 0 . 2,   β = (β 1 ,   β 2 ) t = ( 3 ,   3 ) t ,   μ   = 0,   η 1 ( x 1 )   = 2   sin ( π x 1 )   − 4 / π and   η 2 ( x 2 )   = e x 2 − ( e   − 1 ) ,   ensuring   that     1 0 η 1 ( x )   dx   =   1 0 η 2 ( x )   dx   = 0.   Six   possible   models   combining   different   choices   for   covariates   distributions   were   studied.   For   all   models   X 1 and   X 2 have   marginal   uniform   distribution   but   different   correlation   between   the   covariates   is   allowed.   In   Models   4   to   6,   Z 1 and   Z 2 have   a   discrete   distribution   while   in   Models   1   to   3,   they   correspond   to   continuous   random   variables.   For   clean   samples,   denoted   from   now   on   as   C 0 ,   the   errors   distribution   is   ε ∼ N ( 0 ,   1 ) and   the   considered   models   for   the   covariates   distribution   are

- Model 1: Z 1 , Z 2 , X 1 and X 2 are i.i.d. U ( 0 , 1 ) .
- Model 2: Z 1 , Z 2 , X 1 and X 2 have marginal U ( 0 , 1 ) distribution, X 1 and Z 1 have correlation 0 . 7, while Z 2 and X 2 are independent and independent of Z 1 and X 1.
- Model 3: X 1 and X 2 are i.i.d. U ( 0 , 1 ) and we defined Z 1 = X 1 + X 2 2 + v 1, Z 2 ={ exp ( X 1 ) -1 } / 2 + v 2, where v 1 and v 2 i.i.d. v j ∼ N ( 0 , ( 0 . 1 ) 2 ) .
- Model 4: X 1 and X 2 are i.i.d. with distribution U ( 0 , 1 ) , Z 1 = W 1 / 3 and Z 2 = W 2 / 5 where W 1 ∼ Bi ( 3 , 1 / 2 ) and W 2 ∼ Bi ( 5 , 1 / 5 ) are independent of each other.


• Model   5: X 1 and   X 2 are   i.i.d.   X j ∼ U ( 0 ,   1 ) ,   Z 1 = W 1 / 10 and   Z 2 = W 2 / 10 with   ( W 1 ,   W 2 ,   W 3 )   ∼ M ( 10 ,   1 / 4 ,   1 / 2 ,   1 / 4 ) ,   that   is,   ( W 1 ,   W 2 ,   W 3 ) have   a   joint   multinomial   distribution   of   size   10 and   success   probabilities   1 / 4 ,   1 / 2 and   1 / 4.   Besides,   X 1 ,   X 2 and   ( W 1 ,   W 2 ,   W 3 ) are   independent. Model   6: X and   X are   i.i.d.   X 0   1 ,   Z W 5 with   W Bi 5   1 4 ,   X   X and   W are   independent.   More-

Model 6: X 1 and X 2 are i.i.d. X j ∼ U ( 0 , 1 ) , Z 1 = W 1 / 5 with W 1 ∼ Bi ( 5 , 1 / 4 ) , X 1 , X 2 and W 1 are independent. Moreover, Z 2 = ( 1 / 2 ) { I ( 0 ; 2 / 3 ) ( X 1 ) + W } with W ∼ Bi ( 1 , 1 / 2 ) independent of X 1.

Note that all considered models satisfy the additivity required in assumption N3 . However, under Model 6, h ∗ 2 ( x ) = ( 1 / 2 ) I ( 0 ; 2 / 3 ) ( x 1 ) + ( 1 / 4 ) is not a smooth function.

To study the effect of atypical data on the estimators, we considered three contamination schemes which are described as

- C 1: Ui ∼ 0 . 9 N ( 0 , σ 2 ) + 0 . 1 N ( 0 , 100 σ 2 ) .
- C 2: Ui ∼ 0 . 85 N ( 0 , σ 2 ) + 0 . 15 N ( 15 , 0 . 1 2 ) .


into   9   equally-sized   quadrants   as   shown   in   Fig. 1 .   In   this   scenario   the   errors   are   not   contaminated,   that   is,   ε i ∼ N ( 0 ,   1 ) but   artiﬁcially   9   observations   were   modiﬁed   in   such   a   way   that   ( Z 1 i ,   Z 2 i ) t = ( 20 ,   20 ) t and   its   related   pair   ( X 1 i ,   X 2 i ) t of   covariates   belonged   to   a   different   quadrant   in   the   square   [ 0 ,   1 ]   × [ 0 ,   1 ] .

Contaminations   C 1 and   C 2 correspond   to   vertical   outliers   and   it   is   expected   that   they   will   affect   mainly   the   estimation   of   μ and   eventually   that   of   the   additive   components.   In   particular,   C 1 corresponds   to   a   10% of   the   errors   with   a   larger   variance   producing   an   increase   in   the   mean   square   error   but   not   in   the   bias   of   the   estimates.   In   contrast,   scenario   C 2 corresponds   to   an   asymmetric   gross   error model   in   which   a   15% of   the   errors   are   shifted   in   order   to   produce   “vertical   outliers”   and   will   have   more   effect   on   the   bias.   Finally,   contamination   C 3 aims   to   affect   the   regression   parameter   also   through   the   high–leverage   points   introduced.   Fig. 1 shows   the   100   pairs   of   X i for   one   sample   with   X 1 and   X 2 following   a   U ( 0 ,   1 ) distribution.   The   solid   blue   circles   correspond   to   the   covariates   X i where   Z i has   been   contaminated.   Henceforth,   the   reader   is   referred   to   the   web   version   of   this   article   for   interpretation   of   the   colors   in   the   ﬁgures.   When   no   generated   data   points   X i were   found   in   a   quadrant,   the   observation   Z i was   not   contaminated,   so   the   total   amount   of   contaminated   observations   may   be   smaller   than   9.

Fig. 1. Scatter   plot   of   the   covariates   ( X 1 i ,   X 2 i ) t corresponding   to   one   of   the   samples   contaminated   according   to   scenario   C 3 .   The   solid   circles   indicate   observations   where   ( Z 1 i ,   Z 2 i ) t is   contaminated.   (For   interpretation   of   the   colors   in   the   ﬁgure,   the   reader   is   referred   to   the   web   version   of   this   article.)

For   the   estimators   of   the   regression   parameter   β ,   we   considered   as   summary   measures   the   bias,   standard   deviation   and   the   mean   square   error   of   each   component,   denoted bias , sd and mse ,   respectively.   Tables 1 and   2 report   the   results   for   the   estimators   of   β 1 and   β 2 ,   respectively.                                    

As expected, for clean data sets, the three estimators of β behave similarly (see Tables 1 and 2 ). When estimating the regression   coeﬃcient   β ,   higher   standard   deviations   are   obtained   both   for   the   L 1 and   MM -estimator   due   to   the   loss   of   eﬃciency.   However,   in   most   cases,   the   mean   square   error   of   the   classical   and   MM -methods   are   similar,   since   the   MM estimators   present   smaller   biases.   Note   also   that,   as   in   regression   models,   the   L 1 estimator   is   less   eﬃcient   than   the   MM procedure.   Regarding   the   behavior of   the   estimates   of   β under   contamination,   scenario   C 1 mainly   affects   the   variability   of   the   classical   regression   estimator   (see   Tables 1 and   2 ).   This   performance   is   related   to   the   fact   that   the   errors   are   still   centered   for   this   scheme   but   with   a   larger   dispersion.   The   vertical   asymmetric   outliers   introduced   under   C 2 increase   even   more   the   effect   on   the   variability   of   the   least   squares   procedure   already   observed   under   C 1 ,   leading   to   mean   square   errors   which   are   more   than   600   times   those   obtained   under   C 0 .   Finally,   the   high–leverage   outliers   introduced   under   C 3 have   a   damaging   effect   on   the   classical   estimators   of   β that   become   completely   uninformative,   see   Tables 1 and   2 and   Figs.   S.20   and   S.21   in   the   supplementary   ﬁle,   since   the   absolute   bias   is   enlarged   more   than   800   times   affecting   the   mean   square   error.   A   similar   behavior is   observed   for   the   L 1 estimator,   since   as   mentioned   in   the   introduction   quantiles   estimators   are   related   to   unbounded   loss   functions.   Unlike   the   least   squares   and   L 1 approaches,   the   MM -procedure   is   stable   through   all   considered   contamination   and   models.   The   bias   and   mean   square   error   of   the   estimators   of   β ,   reported   in   Tables 1 and   2 ,   show   that   the   robust   MM -procedure   is   highly   resistant   against   the   contamination   scenarios   considered   here,   even   when   bad   high–leverage   points   are   present   in   the   sample.

Taking   into   account   that   in   Section 4 the   asymptotic   distribution   of   the   MM -regression   estimators   was   obtained   and   to   illustrate   the   asymptotic   performance   of   the   robust   estimates,   we   have   computed   for   each   component   of   β the   mean   over   replications   of   the   estimated   standard   errors,   under   Model   3.   The   results   for   the   other   models   are   given   in   the   supplementary   ﬁle.   These   estimated   standard   errors   were   obtained   according   to   the   expression   for   the   asymptotic   covariance   matrices   given   in   Theorem 4.1 .   When   using   the   classical   estimators,   the   asymptotic   covariance   reduces   to     ( θ ,   σ )   = σ 2 A − 1 .   For   the   least   squares   estimator,   we   computed       evaluating     σ as   the   standard   deviation   of   the   residuals   obtained   from   the   classical   ﬁt   and   the   matrix   A as     A = ( 1 / n )     n i = 1   Z i −   h ∗ ( X i )    Z i −   h ∗ ( X i )   t where     h ∗ minimizes   the   function   ϒ deﬁned   in   ( 18 ).   When   considering   the   MM -estimators,   the   estimators   of   the   asymptotic   variances   were   computed   as   the   diagonal   elements   of       =   B − 1   D   B − 1 following   the   description   to   robustly   estimate   h ∗ given   in   Section 4.1 . Table 3 reports   the   estimated   standard   errors,   denoted as.se ,   and   the   coverage   probability cov.prob for   a   95%   conﬁdence  

interval,   under   Model   3,   for   the   least   squares   and   MM -estimators.   The   coverage   probability   corresponds   to   the   proportion   of   times   that   the   asymptotic   conﬁdence   interval   contains   the   true   parameter.   To   facilitate   comparisons,   we   also   present   the   bias   and   standard   deviation   over   replications   of     β j already   given   in   Tables 1 and   2 .   The   obtained   results   reﬂect   that   the   estimated   standard   errors   are   close   to   the   standard   deviations   obtained   over   replications,   showing   that   the   proposed   procedure   to   estimate   the   asymptotic   covariance   matrix   is   quite   accurate. Fig. 2 presents   the   500   conﬁdence   intervals   obtained,   under   Model   3,   for   when   using   the   classical   and   MM -procedure.  

Fig. 2 presents the 500 confidence intervals obtained, under Model 3, for β 1 when using the classical and MM -procedure. Fig. 2. Conﬁdence intervals for β 1 , under Model 3, obtained when using the classical least squares and MM -procedures.

intervals,   however,   as   illustrated   in   Table 3 the   MM -method   leads   to   a   lower   probability   coverage   than   the   least   squares   one,   in   particular   when   estimating   β 2 .   The   MM -method   developed   in   this   paper   provides   reliable   conﬁdence   intervals   over   the   considered   contamination   settings,   since   their   shape   is   almost   the   same   as   under C 0 and   the   coverage   probability   is   stable.   In   contrast,   even   when   the   coverage   of   the   least   squares   procedure,   under   C 1 and   C 2 ,   is   close   to   that   obtained   for   clean   samples,   this   stability   is   obtained   at   the   expense   of   enlarging   the   conﬁdence   intervals,   specially   under   C 2 .   The   impact   of   high–leverage   points   on   the   bias   of   the   classical   estimators   already   discussed   is   more   evident   when   looking   at   the   conﬁdence   intervals,   since   none   of   them   contain   the   true   value   of   the   parameter.

To   evaluate   the   behavior of   the   additive   component   estimators,   for   j   = 1 ,   2,   as   in   Boente   et   al.   ( 2020b ),   we   measured   the   performance   of   the   estimator     η j of   η j through   the   integrated   squared   error   ( ise )   and   the   squared   integrated   bias.   We   approximate   these   measures   over   an   equally   spaced   grid   of   points   { t   } M   = 1 ,   0   ≤ t 1 < ··· < t M ≤ 1 with   M = 1000,   that   is,   if     η j ,  is   the   estimate   of   the   function   η j obtained   with   the     -th   sample   (1   ≤     ≤ N = 500),   we   computed ise 1 M η ( t ) η ( t ) 2 and Bias 2 1 M   1 N η ( t ) η ( t )   2 .

$$

Taking   into   account   that   a   few   large   values   of   the ise may   have   a   huge   impact   on   its   mean   over   replications   and   to   prevent   us   for   this   distorted   effect,   instead   of   the   mean   integrated   square   error   we   considered   two   measures   less   affected   by   extreme   values:   the   median   of   the ise ,   denoted medise and   the   mean   of   the ise obtained   after   trimming   the   5%   largest   values   labeled 5%-mise .   The   obtained   results   for   the ise are   given   in   Table 4 ,   while   those   regarding   the   squared   integrated   bias   are   summarized   in   Table 5 where   we   report   100   × Bias 2 j . To   have   a   deeper   insight   on   the   performance   of     η 1 and     η 2 ,   Figs. 3 and   4 display   the   obtained   estimated   curves   in   black   dashed   lines,   under   Model   3.   As   it   is   well-known   when   using   splines,   for   both   classical   and   robust   ﬁts,   estimating   problems   may   arise   near   the   boundaries,   for   that   reason,   we   show   here   the   estimates     η 1 and     η 2 evaluated   on   a   grid   of   900   equispaced   points   within   the   interval   [ 0 . 05 ,   0 . 95 ] .   In   addition,   Figs. 5 and   6 give   the   functional   boxplot   of     η 1 and     η 2 ,   also   under   Model   3.   Functional   boxplots,   introduced   by   Sun   and   Genton   ( 2011 ),   are   useful   to   visualize   a   collection   of   curves.   The   area   in   purple   represents   the   50%   inner   band   of   curves,   the   solid   blue   and   the   dotted   red   lines   correspond   to   the   whiskers   and   outlying   curves,   respectively.   Moreover,   the   black   line   indicates   the   deepest   function.   We   also   plot   the   true   functions   in   solid   green   line   to   facilitate   comparisons.

As   appreciated   in   Figs. 3 and   4 ,   under   C 2 ,   the   quantile   procedure   leads   to   one   curve   taking   large   values,   both   when   estimating   η 1 and   η 2 .   For   that   reason,   in   order   to   capture   the   global   behavior of   most   estimates,   in   Figs. 5 and   6 we   have   considered   a   reduced   vertical   axis   (between   −   1 . 5 and   1 . 5)   for   the   L 1 estimator   when   considering   contamination   C 2 .   Analogous   plots   under   Model   6   are   given   in   Figs.   S.7   to   S.10   in   the   supplementary   ﬁle.   Besides,   Figs.   S.11   to   S.18   of   the   supplement   display   the   functional   boxplots   of   the   three   estimates   for   the   other   considered   models.

As   shown   in   Table 4 ,   for   clean   samples,   the 5%-mise and   the medise of   the   three   estimators   exhibit   similar   results.   Moreover,   Figs. 3 to   6 show   that   for   clean   samples,   the   least   squares,   quantile   or   MM -procedure   lead   to   similar   estimates.   However,   it   should   be   noticed   that,   when   considering   the   L 1 method,   the   true   curve   η 1 approaches   to   the   upper   boundary   of   the   50%   inner   band   for   values   close   to   0.5,   explaining   the   larger   bias   reported   in   Table 4 in   this   case.   As   expected,   when   considering   the   functional   boxplots,   the   box   and   whiskers   for   the   MM -ﬁts   are   slightly   larger   than   those   obtained   by   the   classical   approach   due   to   a   loss   of   eﬃciency.

In   contrast,   the   damage   caused   to   the   least   squares   estimators   by   contamination   C 2 on   the ise is   clearly   illustrated   both   in   Table 4 and   in   Figs.   S.3   to   S.6   in   the   supplementary   ﬁle.   For   the   classical   estimators,   the ise of   η 1 and   η 2 ,   across   all   models,   are   larger   than   those   obtained   for   both   the   L 1 or   MM -counterparts.   In   particular,   the 5%-mise or medise of   the   classical   procedure   are   more   than   350   times   those   obtained   with   the   MM -method   in   all   models.   Contamination   C 1 also   affects   the ise of   the   classical   estimators   of   the   additive   components,   but   in   a   smaller   degree   since   the   ratio   between   the   summary   measure   obtained   for   the   classical   and   robust   MM -estimates   is   never   larger   than   10.

It is worth mentioning that contamination C 3 distorts the least squares and L 1 estimators of η 1 and η 2, under Model 3 and those of η 1 under Models 2 and 6, since larger values of the medise are obtained. Furthermore, as reflected in Figs. 5 and 6, the true functions are not contained in the region containing the 50% central curves (see also Figs. S.9 and S.13 in the supplementary fi le). This bias distortion is also reflected on the squared integrated bias, see Table 5. In particular, when estimating η 1, under Models 2, 3 and 6, the mean integrated squared bias is more than 1000 times those obtained under C 0, for both the least squares and L 1 estimators. The worst situation arises under Model 3, there the ratio between mean integrated squared bias of the classical procedure under C 3 and under C 0 is larger than 20000 for ̂ η 1 and almost 8000 when estimating η 2 . A similar conclusion can be obtained for the quantile procedure, showing that in some settings high-leverage points may affect not only the L 1 estimates of the regression parameter β but also those of the additive components. In contrast, when considering the Models 1, 4 and 5, high-leverage outliers have a low impact on the least squares or quantile estimators of η 1 and η 2 only increasing their variability but not affecting their bias which explains the slight increase observed on the 5%-mise and medise (see, for instance, Figs. S.11, S.12 and S.15 to S.18).

Fig. 4. Estimators   of   the   additive   function   η 2 obtained   by   the   least   squares   ( ls ),   quantile   ( l 1 )   and   MM -ﬁt   ( mm ),   under   Model   3.   The   true   function   η 2 is   given   with   a   solid   green   line.

The stability of the MM -proposal under contamination settings C 1 to C 3 is appreciated in Figs. 3 to 6 and S.7 to S.18 as well as in Tables 4 and 5. In particular, the general structure of the additive functions has been better captured by the robust MM -fit which leads to narrower envelopes in the functional boxplots. In most situations, the mean integrated squared bias of the robust MM -estimates is quite similar to those obtained for clean samples. The only exception is when estimating η 2 under Model 1, where the increase on the bias observed under C 2 and C 3 may be explained by the outlying curve appearing in the functional boxplots (see Fig. S.12). In contrast to the described behavior of the least squares or quantile methods, our robust proposal provides more reliable estimates of the additive components under the considered contaminations which generally do not affect them.

# 6.   Real   data   example

In   this   section,   we   analyze the   Boston   housing   data   set,   available   in   the   package MASS in R .   These   data   contain   measurements   from   506   different   houses   taken   at   different   locations   in   Boston   Standard   Metropolitan   Statistical   Area   in   1970   collected   by   the   U.S   Census   Service.   Harrinson   and   Rubinfeld   ( 1978 ) considered   their   median   price   values   and   other   thirteen   socio–demographic   variables   to   evaluate   how   marginal   air   pollution   damages   are   revealed   in   the   housing   market.   Following   Ma   and   Yang   ( 2011 ),   we   modeled the   median   value   of   owner-occupied   homes   in   $1000s,   denoted   MEDV,   with   the   following   four   covariates   of   interest

RM:   average   number   of   rooms   per   dwelling;

TAX:   full-value   property-tax   rate   per   $10,000;

PTRATIO:   pupil-teacher   ratio   by   town   school   district;

LSTAT:   proportion   of   population   that   is   of   “lower   status”   in   %.

These   covariates   were   also   considered   in   Wang   and   Yang   ( 2009 ) who   ﬁt   an   additive   model   to   these   data.   Ma   and   Yang   ( 2011 ) considered   a   partially   linear   additive   model   with   a   linear   component   on   the   pupil–teach   ratio.   For   that   reason,   we   also   propose   to   ﬁt   the   model

$$
M E D V = \mu + \beta P R T A I O + \eta _ { 1 } ( R M ) + \eta _ { 2 } ( \log ( T A X ) ) + \eta _ { 3 } ( \log ( L S T A ) ) + \sigma \, \varepsilon \, , \\ \\ \log ( \mu ) = \mu + \beta P R T A I O + \eta _ { 1 } ( R M ) + \eta _ { 2 } ( \log ( T A X ) ) + \eta _ { 3 } ( \log ( L S T A ) ) + \sigma \, \varepsilon \, , \\
$$

where   the   errors   ε are   assumed   to   be   independent,   independent   of   the   covariates,   with   symmetric   distribution   and   scale   1.   With   the   notation   given   in   equation   ( 2 ),   Z = PTRATIO and   X   = ( X 1 ,   X 2 ,   X 3 ) t = ( RM ,   log ( TAX ),   log ( LSTAT )) t . To   estimate   the   additive   components,   we   use   cubic   B -splines.   When   estimating   η j ,   the   knots   are   taken   as   the    /( k   +

Both   the   classical   and   the   robust   BIC criteria   introduced   in   ( 8 ) selected   k   = 5 to   approximate   the   additive   functions.   When   using   the   robust   procedure,   the   loss   functions   and   their   tuning   constants   were   selected   as   in   the   simulation   study.

The   curves   given   in   Fig. 7 illustrate   the   differences   existing   between   the   robust   and   classical   estimates   of   the   additive   components.   In   particular,   the   classical   estimate   of   the   additive   component   η 1 ,   related   to   RM,   has   a   lower   peak   than   the   robust   one   for   high   values   of   RM.   Besides,   the   curve   takes   larger   values   for   the   classical   ﬁt   than   for   the   robust   one   when   RM   varies   between   4 and   6 . 5.   Similarly,   the   classical   estimate   of   η 2 is   below   the   robust   one   for   values   smaller   than   6 . 1,   while   those   related   to   the   logarithm   of   LSTAT   are   above   the   robust   estimate   when   the   covariate   takes   values   smaller   than   2.

The   presence   of   possible   atypical   data   is   suspected   from   the   differences   arising   between   the   estimations   of   the   additive   components   and   the   regression   parameter.   To   identify   these   atypical   observations,   we   use   the   residuals   obtained   from   the   robust   ﬁt.   Their   boxplot   displayed   in   Fig. 8 identiﬁes   as   outliers   the   21   atypical   observations   labeled 8,   158,   182,   215,   227,   233,   343,   365,   369,   370,   371,   372,   373,   376,   381,   392,   408,   409,   410,   454   and   506,   representing   around   a   4%   of   the   whole   sample.   We   repeat   the   analysis   using   the   classical   procedure,   after   removing   these   potential   atypical   data.   From   now   on,   we   indicate   with   the   upper   script   ( − out ) the   classical   estimates   obtained   without   these   21   observations. As   with   the   full   data   set,   the   classical   BIC   selected   the   same   number   of   knots,   i.e.,   k   = 5,   for   all   components.   The   dashed  

As with the full data set, the classical BIC selected the same number of knots, i.e., k = 5, for all components. The dashed red lines in Fig. 9 correspond to ̂ η ( -out ) j , ls , that is, to the classical fi t computed without the potential outliers. For comparison, we also plot, in blue solid lines, the robust estimators already displayed in Fig. 7.

  behavior to   that   of   the   least   squares   method,   if   one   were   able   to   manually   eliminate   the   atypical   observations.   Besides,   the   classical   estimate   of   μ after   removing   the   outliers   equals     μ ( − out ) ls = 37 . 247,   which   gives   a   value   closer   to     μ mm than   that   obtained   with   the   whole   sample.   The   resulting   value   of     β ( − out ) ls and   its   estimated   asymptotic   standard   deviation   are   reported   in   the   third   column   of   Table 7 .   Note   that   the   asymptotic   conﬁdence   interval   based   on     β mm with   level   0.99   equals   [− 0 . 5483 ,   − 0 . 4715 ] which   does   not   include   the   classical   estimate   obtained   with   the   whole   data   set,   but   includes   the   one   obtained   after   the   detected   outliers   have   been   removed.   It   is   worth   mentioning   that   the   estimated   standard   deviation   of   the   classical   estimator   after   removing   the   observations   is   still   quite   large   compared   to   that   obtained   for   the   robust   procedure.

This   fact   may   be   explained   since   the   identiﬁed   outliers   do   not   correspond   to   high–leverage   data,   that   is,   with   atypical   values   on   the   pupil-teacher   ratio   which   still   inﬂuence   the   estimates   of   the   matrix   A .

To   conclude,   as   also   shown   in   the   simulation   study,   this   example   illustrates   the   fact   that   the   least   squares   approach   leads   to   estimators   seriously   affected   by   atypical   observations.   In   contrast,   the   robust   procedure   gives   more   reliable   results   automatically   down–weighting   potential   outliers.   The   robust   ﬁt   also   allows   to   identify   potential   atypical   observations   and   leads   to   estimated   additive   components     η j that   are   almost   identical   to   those   obtained   with   the   classical   procedure   applied   to   the   data   set   without   the   detected   outliers.    

# 7. Final comments

In   this   paper,   we   presented   a   procedure   to   robustly   estimate   the   regression   parameter   and   the   additive   components   under   a   partially   linear   additive   model.   The   method   combines   B -splines   to   smoothly   estimate   the   additive   components   and   MM -estimators.   The   use   of   B -splines   avoids   the   higher   complexity   of   kernels   methods   which   require   backﬁtting   or   marginal   integration   combined   with   a   proﬁle   approach,   see,   for   instance,   Li   ( 2000 ) or   Ma   and   Yang   ( 2011 ) for   a   discussion   on   the   computational   problems   arising   in   partially   linear   additive   model   when   using   kernel   methods.   In   contrast   to   Ma   and   Yang   ( 2011 ),   we   do   not   consider   an   additional   step   in   the   procedure   using   kernel   smoothers   to   obtain   estimators   of   the   nonparametric   components   for   which   the   limiting   distribution   can   be   derived.   However,   our   proposal   can   be   extended   to   provide   a   robust   spline-backﬁtted   kernel   smoother   combining   the   ideas   in   Ma   and   Yang   ( 2011 ) to   construct   pseudo– observations   with   the   robust   kernel   smoothers   deﬁned   in   Boente   and   Fraiman   ( 1989 ).   This   important   issue   is   beyond   the   scope   of   the   paper   and   will   be   object   of   future   work.

For   the   robust   B -spline   MM -estimators   proposed   consistency   results   and   rates   of   convergence   are   obtained.   Besides,   the   asymptotic   distribution   of   the   linear   regression   estimator   is   derived   under   mild   conditions.   The   numerical   results   illustrate   the   stability   of   the   proposed   methods   under   the   considered   contaminations   and   models.   We   exemplify   our   proposal   on   the   well   known   Boston   housing   data   set.   The   analysis   shows   that   the   robust   MM -estimators   automatically   discard   inﬂuential   observations   leading   to   reliable   estimates.

An   important   topic   to   be   discussed   is   the   feasibility   of   the   procedure   when   the   number   of   additive   components   is   large.   Even   though,   in   linear   regression   models,   Smucler   ( 2019 ) derived   consistency   results   for   S and   MM -regression   estimators   when   the   ratio   between   the   number   of   covariates   and   the   sample   size   converges   to   0,   it   is   well   known   that   the   number   of   subsamples   needed   to   obtain   good   candidates   for   the   S -estimator   increases   rapidly   with   the   number   of   covariates   and   that   the   estimator   becomes   unreliable   if   only   a   small   number   of   candidates   is   used,   see   Section   5.7.4   in   Maronna   et   al.   ( 2019 ).   Therefore,   under   a   partial   additive   linear   model,   when   p is   large   and   taking   into   account   that   the   total   number   of   parameters   may   be   even   larger   due   to   the   basis   dimension,   the   computation   of   the   scale   estimator   which   uses   an   S estimator   may   be   impractical.   As   described   in   Maronna   et   al.   ( 2019 ),   an   alternative   is   to   use   the   outlier   detection   method   introduced   in   Peña   and   Yohai   ( 1999 ) to   compute   the   initial   scale   estimator.   For   large   values   of   p and   q ,   some   penalization   as   the   Ridge,   LASSO   or   SCAD   penalty   will   be   needed   in   order   to   make   the   estimators   computable.   One   possibility   is   to   penalize   the   objective   function   in   ( 6 ) by   adding   a   penalization   on   the   regression   parameters   if   β is   assumed   to   be   sparse   and   a   group   LASSO   method   for   selecting   the   coeﬃcients   of   the   spline   approximations,   as   done   in   Huang   et   al.   ( 2010 ) for   additive   models.   The   study   of   such   robust   penalized   estimators   is   an   interesting   and   challenging   topic,   that   will   be   object   of   future   work.

A.1.   Proofs   of   Proposition 3.1 and   Theorem 3.2

Lemmas A.1 to   A.5 will   be   needed   to   prove   Proposition 3.1 and   Theorem 3.2 .   In   particular,   Lemma A.1 ,   whose   proof   can   be   found   in   the   supplementary   ﬁle,   regards   the   Fisher-consistency   of   the   proposed   estimators.   Fisher   consistency   guarantees   that   we   are   estimating   the   target   quantities   and   is   a   ﬁrst   step   to   obtain   consistency   results.

- (ii) If in addition C6 holds, (µ, β , η 1 , . . . , η p ) is the unique minimizer of L ( a , b , g 1 , . . . , gp , ς) .
